### SORCERY

Shaping Ritual: On the first shape sorcery action to begin casting a spell, stunt the use of Grandmother in spellcasting to gain (stunt rating + 2) sorcerous motes toward completing that spell. Once per scene; the wielder’s control spell doesn’t count against this limit.

### The Gouged Eye Society

Background: Sorcerers, diabolists, geomancers. The secretive members of the Gouged Eye Society band together to share lore, pursue profit, and protect one another from their occasionally unhinged feats of genius. More than anything else, the Society exists to seek dangerous and necessary paths to power; they see themselves as the champions of mortal-kind, working to resist the hegemony of the Exalted — Dragon Blooded and Anathema alike.

Three Overseers manage the Society’s affairs: Isabet Maken of the Cauldron Valley (p. XX), a master geomancer; Grinning San, an accountant turned demonologist; and Mol Skarre, an alchemist without peer. From their manse, the Weeping Monolith, they administrate a network of associates who smuggle heretical texts, dangerous reagents, and other perilous treasures throughout the region and Creation at large. The Guild remains unaware of (or at least indifferent to) the Society and its machinations. To preserve their secrecy, the Gouged Eye conducts its business through an excessively elaborate web of intermediaries that could baffle even the most dedicated magistrate. While a number of spirits, Exalts, and others are dimly aware of the Society, the Overseers have been broadly successful in maintaining a low profile, especially given their considerable resources and outsized schemes. Members: Membership in the Society is open to the brilliant and the broken; unfulfilled ambition is the common denominator of the Society at large. Like Isabet and Grinning San, many of the members come

from the ranks of the Guild, but they are also drawn from local craft-lodges, priesthoods, and syndicates. New initiates are often driven to join for political reasons as well as practical ones — their enthusiasm for their particular field of study usually borders on mania, limiting their ability to secure support from other institutions.

Only true mortals are allowed entrance into the Gouged Eye Society, although the Overseers and membership have a long-standing dialogue about what constitutes a “true” mortal. After a period of indentured service to the Society (or a significant donation), the Overseers brand novices with the hook-and-circle symbol of the Gouged Eye. Members swear themselves to the Society, the Overseers, and to mortal-kind itself, in that order. While members have access to the Society’s resources in conducting their experiments, this access is limited by a system of adversarialism, with a randomly chosen colleague allowed to review potential projects, offer public comments on their merit, and even challenge their value to the Gouged Eye. In this way, the membership governs itself with the appearance of a meritocracy, even if the system is anything but. The Overseers often play members against one another, especially in “randomly” choosing a project’s reviewer. Despite these stressors, the Gouged Eye Society is unflinchingly loyal to its members: When a novice conjures a demon beyond their abilities, it is the Society’s sorcerers who banish or bind it; when a local don demands his share in a savant’s profits, it is the Society’s own enforcers

EX3

who make a show of strength. In short: The members are united by frustration at a world that does not understand that obsession is the mother of discovery.

The Weeping Monolith

(Greater Manse)

Secrets: This is not the first Gouged Eye Society. Its symbol recurs throughout recorded history as a mark for savants and occultists throughout the eight directions. Only the Overseers are aware of this mystery; they suspect the Weeping Monolith itself lures ambitious outcasts to satiate its own hunger for knowledge. They desperately search for members of the previous Society, which fell into obscurity almost two centuries ago.

A greater manse of indeterminate aspect, the Weeping Monolith is a perfect cube of blood-colored marble large enough to dwarf even the palaces of Ysyr. A single ruby the size of a child is embedded on the cube’s top, producing a slow but steady flow of colorless oil that coats the manse in a sickly sheen. The lion’s share of the manse’s power is used to shroud it from unwelcome attention. Despite being a fortress-sized block on a treeless hillside, it is all but invisible to anyone except attuned initiates of the Gouged Eye Society; others must pay one Willpower when making a Survival or Investigation roll or suffer a -5 penalty to any attempt to locate the manse.

Resources: The Gouged Eye Society controls formidable holdings of land, wealth, and lore, but its foremost resources are demesnes and manses. Isabet Maken’s Cauldron Valley is a powerful demesne that reshapes those who dwell within; her mutant slaves form the base of the Society’s workforce and funding. A handful of members maintain lesser demesnes, exporting useful resources to stock the Society’s laboratories or put silver in its coffers.

At the center of the Society is the Weeping Monolith, a manse of unknown aspect and providence that seems to actively resist all investigation. Inside, the Gouged Eye Society has amassed a truly staggering collection of texts, including accounts of obscure medical procedures, conflicting hierarchies of the demon princes, and fragmented histories from the dawn of mortalkind. Beyond the allure of its libraries, the Society quickly raises any interested member out of poverty by connecting them with useful patrons, resources, and information. It is trivial for any member of the Gouged Eye to attain Resources 2 — and most live in even greater luxury within the Weeping Monolith or under the auspices of their senior colleagues. Roleplaying Hints: The Gouged Eye Society is not a singular body; it includes specialists with diverse talents and interests who nonetheless work for their common benefit. Interfering in the affairs of any given member will bring a character to the attention of the Society overall, and while the Society rarely acts directly or immediately, the character will be subjected to scrutiny, especially in commerce and occult dealings. Although they mistrust the Exalted, the Gouged Eye is happy to patronize any useful patsies, tempting collaborators with arcane knowledge and easy wealth. If discovered, the Society quickly closes rank and regroups. They have not yet faced a significant breach of their secrecy, and the reaction from the membership and Overseers alike would be erratic, or even desperate.

Inside, the Monolith is a puzzlebox of rooms of irregular size and shape, connected in ways that beggar geometry and confound all attempts at mapping. In addition to usual benefits of Essence respiration, the Weeping Monolith rewards those who serve it: Adding new texts to the manse’s collection is as refreshing as a good meal and a stout drink; particularly valuable additions can send someone into spasms of phantom pleasure.

The Tear That Blinds

(•••, Greater Hearthstone)

Keywords: Dependent

The sigil of the Gouged Eye Society pulses slowly inside this dark ruby. The bearer is invisible to the effects of scrying and prophecy, unless backed by power comparable to Solar Circle Sorcery.

Mol Skarre

Although she is the owner of the Weeping Monolith and bears its hearthstone, Mol Skarre defers to Isabet (and, to a lesser extent, Grinning San), preferring to busy herself with increasingly elaborate experimentation in sorcerous alchemy. Despite her fascination with the countless ways the body and mind can bend under the weight of the right drug, she is uncompromisingly ascetic and never personally partakes, instead inflicting her experiments on junior members, slaves, and other unwitting subjects. She is currently preparing to flood Great Forks with bravura, a sorcerous drug that renders a user incapable of fear. This will be her first large-scale test of its efficacy…and its withdrawal symptoms.

### SORCERY

Shaping Ritual: Mol Skarre initiated into the mysteries of sorcery through the completion of many austerities, but the most important was a fasting ritual where she subsisted on a broth of pine needles.

At the start of a fast, roll (permanent Willpower + Integrity); Mol Skarre can endure a number of days equal to (successes + Stamina). At the conclusion, she gains 10 sorcerous motes, plus one for each day endured. These remain available until she begins a new fast.

Kiss of Fatal Courage (Cost: 15sm, 1wp; One day; Psyche; Control spell): The sorcerer conjures one of the Myth-winds that carries legends of Creation’s bravest heroes and catches it inside her lungs. The sorcerer is effectively mute for the duration, but anyone directly exposed to her breath is enchanted. She rolls 8 dice against the target’s Resolve (characters with this spell roll Intelligence + Occult); if successful, her victim loses all capacity for fear for the next full day, treating social influence to that effect as unacceptable, except for the Dawn Caste anima power. Battle groups afflicted by this spell have perfect morale. Subjects who resist the spell’s effect are immune to it for a week, but those who succumb develop an addiction to fearlessness — they gain a minor Tie driving them to experience it again. A sorcerer who knows Kiss of Fatal Courage as their control spell is capable of crafting an alchemical equivalent known as bravura, requiring a week of effort and success on a Difficulty 3 Craft (Alchemy) or Medicine roll. This produces 3 doses for every threshold success.

Grinning San

The most socially adept of the Society’s Overseers, Grinning San is a tiny man with a head for numbers and a knack for

EX3

bargaining with the denizens of the Demon Realm. Despite his own humble beginnings as a Guild accountant, San has proven himself to be capable, confident, and surprisingly convincing. Tragically, he was born without a personality; his voice is flat and stony, and his demeanor is equally rigid. His current passion project is the vivisection of a raksha cataphract, assisted by his neomah advisors. That several villages have been wiped off the map by Fair Folk raiders in search of their lost brother does not bother San, except insofar as the raids seem to be on track to reach the Monolith if they are allowed to continue unabated.

### SORCERY

Shaping Ritual: San found enlightenment in the dizzying monotony of a ledger, glimpsing the equation by which all of Creation is ordered. With a few hours of calculation and an (Intelligence + Lore) roll, he may bank sorcerous motes equal to his successes until he next attempts to pin down the ineffable mathematics of existence. Each subsequent attempt inflicts a cumulative −1 penalty to later attempts; this resets at the start of a new story. Demon of the First Circle (Cost: Ritual, 2wp; Instant): Grinning San is usually accompanied by a pair of bound neomah (Exalted, p.525). Ineffable Koan (Cost: 15sm, 1wp;One scene; Psyche; Control Spell): In a trance, the sorcerer poses an impossible riddle that burns in the air above. Anyone who catches sight of the riddle or hears its initial recitation is driven to seek an answer, and must flurry to take any action other than puzzling it out. In combat, thinking about the riddle is a difficulty 1 (Intelligence + Lore) extended action, with a goal number of (the sorcerer’s Lore + Essence). Outside of combat, each roll requires a minute of careful deduction. In either case, characters may spend one Willpower to make an immediate, reflexive roll on their turn. Sorcerers with Ineffable Koan as their control spell can ritually inscribe a koan on a physical surface with a few minutes of effort; these riddles last until the next sunrise.

### The Graveyard Kettle, Far-Roaming Beast Hunters

The Graveyard Kettle,

Far-Roaming Beast Hunters

Background: Known as bandits, poachers, and occasional folk heroes, the Graveyard Kettle are a vagrant hunting caravan who escape prosecution through gifts of exotic pelts and ivory to the rich and powerful. Led by Mwaka Galbi, a god-blooded hunter who abandoned his homeland, the Kettle receives lucrative commissions to slay dangerous monsters, capture exotic beasts, or prepare mythic trophies. The Graveyard Kettle’s original members were once the royal guards of Uasaka and its queen, Mwaka’s half-sister Chausa. Their godly mother tried to Exalt Chausa with a stolen spark of Exigence, but the flame tore the queen’s body and soul asunder, erupting into a cataclysm that consumed hundreds. Seizing on the ensuing chaos, Uasakan powerbrokers and Imperial missionaries slandered Chausa as a heretic and claimed the throne for a Nellens puppet-prince. Mwaka and his fellow guards left or were driven out, depending on the telling, and have lived by wit, skill, and infamy ever since.

Ironically, their growing fame is also the source of the Kettle’s current woes. Uasaka’s puppet-prince is convinced Mwaka will seek to claim his sister’s throne. He sends provocateurs to spread dark rumors about the Kettle, backs Guild-affiliated hunting bands to compete for contracts, and is courting an Outcaste assassin as a last resort. To their credit, the Kettle endures in the face of these struggles — even if Mwaka’s wife only barely convinced him to not to return one of Uasaka’s rival hunting bands to their employer as leather.

Members: The core of the Graveyard Kettle is an elite band, bound by camaraderie and experience rather than a strict hierarchy, who use the traits of medium cavalry (Exalted, p. 497), and keep claw striders (Exalted, p. 561) as mounts. The band recruits ambitious youths and the orphans of monster attacks, training them in hunting tactics in exchange for camp labor.

EX3

Mwaka Galbi is the Kettle’s founder, quick to laugh and easygoing despite a life of hardship. He leads without commanding, having earned the trust of every Kettle hunter a dozen times over. If he is both the brains and brawn of the Kettle, his wife Pembe is certainly its stomach, digesting its kills into the money and influence the caravans need to survive. Alongside Mwaka and Pembe, the Kettle’s innermost circle is composed of Uasakan guards, fighters, and others who followed Mwaka into exile: stout-bellied Pili, meticulous Shumari, ice-eyed Nassor, and half a dozen others besides. Beyond them, dozens of hunters have joined or travel with the Kettle. Orphans taken in as camp followers often graduate into proper membership when they come of age. Secrets: The Kettle’s oldest trophy is a huge canine skull of bone-jade, scavenged from an ancient den. It currently serves as scaffolding for the lead caravan, but a powerful necromancer could use it to raise a behemoth war-beast fit to ravage armies.

Though he is loath to use it, Mwaka knows where to find the red-opal prison of the Burning Antelope Princess. She would cut a swathe of fiery desolation for hundreds of miles if released — and as Mwaka’s frustration with Uasaka’s usurper grows so too does this temptation. Resources: The Graveyard Kettle travel in six caravans drawn by Wyld-beasts, each a temple to one aspect of their art: tanning, butchering, corralling, and so on. The hunters wear hide armor, for mobility is their best protection, but equip themselves with shell-crushing clubs, feather-pinning arrows, or hide-piercing spears as appropriate. Each also has a personal weapon that incorporates relics from their greatest kill, as much a work of sacred art as a killing tool, the greatest of which is Mwaka’s fang-studded grimcleaver, Kula.

friend and an unflappable enemy who serves as a mentor and sounding board for many of the Kettle’s younger members. Image: Now entering his eighties, Shumari is a dark, lanky man made of gristle and scar tissue. His long, graying beard is tied in an array of braids and ornamented with beads. Still unbowed by age, he moves light and spry as an oryx, eyes twinkling with vigilance. Intimacies: Defining: Mwaka (Loyalty); Major: “Act with deliberation, not haste”; Minor: The Kettle’s Orphans (Grudging Affection), Uasaka (Longing)

Secrets: Shumari was envenomed by a shadow-rot manticore in his last hunt, and the wound weeps with black, purulent sludge. He uses stolen thaumaturgical medicine to keep the venom in check, unwilling to reveal his infirmity to Mwaka for fear of being forced aside. Equpment: Shumari wears thicker hide than most, and fights with a horn-tipped spear and leather buckler. He still wears his saffron cloak, a sign of his former office among the royal guard, though it is long since frayed and torn. Roleplaying Hints: Shumari has the easy confidence of age without bitterness or judgment. When he offers advice, it is always practical and specific, for he has little patience for things cryptic and philosophical. He will bear any burden that a friend needs of him, even to his own detriment… but he cannot do so forever. He is near his breaking point, feeling (and fearing) the first pangs of infirmity.

Roleplaying Hints: The Kettle began as a self-imposed exile, and its older hunters carry themselves with stoic dignity, emphasizing the spiritual, ritual aspects of the hunt. Younger hunters don’t share this history, and are prone to boasting. Both regard pride as paramount, however, and never forget a quarry that escaped or forced them to flee. Outsiders seldom anticipate how fast a perceived challenge to the Kettle’s reputation can escalate into deadly competition, but their respect is invaluable once earned.

Her story is common among the Kettle’s younger hunters, who flock to Mwaka for strength, protection, vengeance, and purpose.

Sanura’s mount, Mouse, uses the normal statistics for a claw strider (Exalted, p. 561), except he has Willpower 7, Resolve 4, three additional dice for combat movement, and can execute disarm and distract gambits.

Among her peers, Sanura is well-regarded as a competent archer and hunter, but a truly exceptional rider whose bonded mount — a sand-colored clawstrider she calls Mouse — can run down even the fastest prey. She plays mentor to even some of the Kettle’s senior hunters in the finer points of strider-mounted archery, teaching them to move as one being.

Image: Sanura is a dark, lean woman raked with scars from a hardscrabble childhood. She keeps her black hair shorn almost to the scalp, and dresses to blend in with the sand and savannah. Her claw strider, Mouse, is half a head taller than her, with a crest of bright bronze ridges and a lavishly embroidered saddle.

Intimacies: Defining: Mouse (Devotion); Major: “Preparation is the antidote for calamity”; Minor: The Graveyard Kettle (Loyalty), Animals (Easy Rapport) Secrets: The hunters of the Kettle do not know that Mouse was stolen from the stables of Thunderswift, a god-blooded merchant-prince of considerable wealth. With a bounty out for the claw strider, Sanura cannot keep this secret much longer, and will likely entangle the Graveyard Kettle in a long, messy skirmish with Thunderswift to everyone’s detriment.

Equpment: Sanura wears a jerkin of hellboar hide and is never seen without her yew bow and a full quiver. Her few adornments are bronze, to match Mouse’s scales. She is training several camp followers in husbandry and riding; they follow in tow with her impressive array of tack for claw striders, horses, camels — even rare beasts, like simhata.

Tactics

Shumari, Sanura, and the other hunters of the Kettle are trained in several specialized gambits:

Bind (difficulty 2-4)

The character locks down her opponent’s movement by tangling them in the environment or skewering their robe, shoe, or tail with a spare weapon. The gambit’s target is grappled (Exalted, p. 200). However, the character is not grappling them, and does not make a control roll.

Roleplaying Hints: Sanura projects gentle confidence, but is still uncertain in her authority as a full member of the Graveyard Kettle. She is uncomfortable giving orders, especially to the young camp followers — after all, she was one until very recently.

Instead, a pinned character or nearby ally can use a miscellaneous action to free themselves with a (Strength + Athletics or Larceny) roll. The difficulty is (2 + extra successes on the gambit attack roll), and lowers by one with each unsuccessful attempt.

This hesitation vanishes in her areas of specialty. She is the undisputed master of the Kettle’s corrals, confidently correcting even Mwaka when he pushes a mount too

This gambit is difficulty 3 at short range, and difficulty 4 at long range. A grapple gambit targeting a bound character costs only 1 Initiative.

A successful riposte gambit allows the character to prepare a devastating counterblow. If the gambit’s target attacks the character before her next turn, she can make a reflexive, decisive clash attack against it. This attack does not count as her combat action, and adds four dice to its damage pool.

Pillars of the Last Bastion,

Cult of Aton

Background: Over the centuries, the Last Mountain (see Unrelenting Aton, the Last Mountain, p. XX) has gathered a city of worshipers in his subterranean fortress. Those who share Aton’s belief that the end of days approaches live out their lives in the Last Bastion as Aton’s Pillars, devoting themselves to his survival and their lasting legacy.

To prepare their best defenses, the Pillars need artifacts, magic, surface resources, and all the information they can amass about the coming cataclysm. A select conclave of volunteers ventures to the surface every few months, armed with a purpose that ranges from general recruitment to the acquisition of some particular resource or secret. The cult has no desire to make itself widely known, but a scroll posted on the walls of a Prasadi city or a missionary speaking on a street corner in Palanquin rarely attracts attention. The people of the Dreaming Sea believe the Pillars of the Last Bastion to be just one of many tiny cults, unaware of the legion that lurks below the earth.

A conclave aiming to steal, assassinate, or subvert first acts as preachers raving about the coming apocalypse, gathering information, and biding their time. A handful of mortal cultists, dismissed and ignored as foolish or delusional by the authorities, can advance all sorts of plans: bribing a farmer to redirect a share of fresh crops, locating and stealing a magical resource, or seducing or kidnapping a trained artisan to serve the city below. Those caught simply play the part of the lone zealot, and take the secret of Aton’s true might to their grave. When a conclave encounters insurmountable opposition, they send for Aton’s Fists. Aton does not grant power lightly, but he arms and blesses a select few of his most devoted followers to serve as enforcers, battering rams, and occasional distractions. Each Fist expects to die young in service to his cause and to his friends and family below, and carries no marks of affiliation. Members: The Pillars of the Last Bastion recruit throughout the Dreaming Sea, and as far north as Ember

EX3

and Nexus. They target the weary, the wounded, and the dispossessed: those inclined to believe the cult’s apocalyptic message. Recruiters also haunt places of learning, promising community and a cause to bright young minds in search of purpose. More rarely, they target a powerful individual whom Aton deems worth a risk. Recruiters prefer to work slowly, allowing potentials to draw themselves into the cult’s web, but Aton hardly cares whether his followers have free will. When the Last Bastion’s numbers dwindle, he sends conclaves to acquire new worshipers at any cost, and forces loyalty with blackmail, threats, and torture. Though the imminent end of the world prompts few Pillars to start a family, a small number of cultists are born each year in the Last Bastion. Aton trusts such natives deeply, often tutoring them himself as personal servants, as those indoctrinated from birth are the least likely to pine for a life outside.

The Last Bastion has only a limited official hierarchy, composed of a few dozen officials who oversee food production and other resource management. The ambitious are more likely to seek power and prestige through the conclaves than through city service. The cult applauds Pillars who return from the surface with magic, contacts, or new recruits, celebrating them as holy heroes. Those who survive many conclaves, repeatedly risking death or capture for their prizes, become unofficial priests. Cultists believe their devotion and success grants them great spiritual wisdom. Since successful conclaves are often long-term affairs, taking months or years to complete, most of Aton’s priests are also the community’s elders.

Equally revered are Aton’s Fists, whom the Last Mountain elevates with his blessings. Turning slowly and inevitably into immovable stone, the Fists live like princes for the duration of their shortened lives, rewarded for their service with the finest surface-world luxuries money can buy. In exchange, Aton expects perfect loyalty, and a willingness to die for the Pillars without hesitation.

Secrets: Some heretics among the Pillars think the world can still be saved. Aton crushes such hopes wherever his Fists and other devotees find them, killing those he cannot convince. Still, he has never been able to completely destroy the idea that the Last Bastion and its resources might be better put toward staving off the end, rather than merely surviving it. Rumor holds that the hopeful include over a dozen members of successful conclaves, and at least three Fists. Aton would do much to learn the identities of his city’s apostates. In turn, they fervently

wish someone would come along who can convince their god to abandon his nihilism and turn his power toward changing the world — or maybe even saving it. Resources: The Last Bastion is a settlement of over 10,000 people, the majority of whom work tirelessly for the cause. The city is a mile underground, accessible through a handful of hidden, guarded tunnels that surface far from major settlements between Y’danna and Palanquin. Scavenged First Age devices keep the tunnels lit and provide for subsistence farming and manufacturing, supplemented by whatever the conclaves can send back. New recruits, who donate their life’s savings in exchange for Aton’s protection, pay for additional supplements and specialized goods to be purchased in secret from the surface. City guards carry quality weapons and armor, and every grown citizen can be armed and drafted into the Last Bastion’s army in times of emergency. It is considered pious for citizens to own only the most basic and necessary personal belongings; Aton and his Fists are the only denizens of the city who own other property.

Surrounding Aton’s inner sanctum is a tremendous library, full of not just books and scrolls but also rare reagents and carefully cataloged magical objects. A handful of scholar-cultists and thaumaturges assist him in studying these prizes, predicting exactly how and when the world will end, and what more they can do to survive it. Aton’s apocalypse library contains surprisingly detailed information on the Fair Folk, the horrors of the First Age, the rumored Abyssal Exalted, and myriad other strange threats. Roleplaying Hints: Conclaves are full of ambitious people, the city’s best and brightest. Most firmly adhere to Aton’s nihilistic views, but expect their actions will ensure either their survival or some lasting legacy. Others are simply eager for a chance to gain respect and power over others — their greed fuels their fervor. Fists tend to be fanatical; having sacrificed most of their lifespan to serve their god, most find it difficult to contemplate whether Aton’s dire predictions might be wrong. In the city itself, and sometimes among conclaves that spend years away from the Last Bastion, people are less certain. Aton has been proclaiming the end of days for centuries now, yet Creation survives. Heretical cultists seek to influence their god or steal his secrets, while priests fight over material power in their would-be dying city. Many citizens are simply frightened, desperate to live another day.

To all his followers, Aton is not a mere elemental, but a mighty god with no equal, worthy of fear and respect. They

EX3

are disinclined to believe the Last Mountain could ever be bested or destroyed, even by other powerful beings, and are wary of outsiders promising hope and change.

Thaumaturgy Ritual:

Summon the Mountain (••)

The Last Bastion’s most trusted priests pass down a ritual nearly as old as Unrelenting Aton himself. A conclave consisting of five people — one of whom must be a Fist — gathers to summon Aton. The participants intone prophecies from Aton’s apocalypse library while the priest chisels one such prophecy into the Fist’s stonelike skin. The ritual takes three hours to complete. Opponents may attempt to interrupt the summoning, though wise conclaves post sentries (often comprising a mob of newly recruited cultists) to protect the ritualists from would-be disruptors. Changing or destroying the symbols carved in the Fist’s flesh ends the ritual. The conclave must wait a week before attempting it again.

The Fist’s death causes Aton to dissipate as a miscellaneous action and reform in his cavern in the Last Bastion. Aton may also willingly dissipate to return home at any time. If Aton is destroyed while summoned, his followers’ prayers allow him to reform in his cavern over the course of one week, unless he was slain with spirit-killing magic such as the Solar Charm Ghost-Eating Technique (Exalted, p.354).

Marru Charrin

The eldest of Aton’s still-moving Fists, Marru is in her mid50s, with stone skin pocked from battle. She received the Blessing of Aton long ago and has been a loyal Fist ever since, but rumor holds that her only daughter, now missing, is a heretic. Aton would usually kill a Fist over such allegations, but Marru is useful, popular, and old for one with his blessing. He hopes she will soon find her end in battle or petrify fully rather than having to die at his hands.

If the ritual is successful, Aton appears a short distance from the summoner with a mighty rumbling, though is not otherwise bound to her will.

EX3

Essence: 1; Willpower: 8; Join Battle: 8 dice

Health Levels: −0x14/−1x4/−2x4/−4x2/Incap. Note: Medicine pools targeting her can’t exceed the healer’s highest relevant stone-based Craft Ability, and healing Charms targeting her double their costs unless they have an earthen elemental nature.

### EXAMPLE ACTIVE CONCLAVES

A Dragon-Blooded warrior in Prasad has lost everything: her family, her lover, her honor. She would make a fine addition to the Last Bastion, if the Pillars can convince her to join. Two recruiters now work in the palace: one as a maid who whispers in the Exalt’s ear, the other as a messenger who controls what letters she receives. Outside the walled city, a handful of Aton’s Fists work to ensure her life continues on its downward spiral. Palanquin’s gateway intrigues Aton. He envies the heavenly gods safely hidden on the other side, away from the Fair Folk and Exalted. He sends a conclave of scholars to study it, and perhaps find a way to bring their patron to Yu-Shan, oblivious to the fact that the ancient realm of Zen-Mu lies beyond instead. One among their number is not as loyal to Aton as he claims to be.

Aton once extracted a promise from Tenepeshu, who lurks beneath the bay of Champoor: 1,000 mortals sent over 100 years to serve Tenepeshu in darkness beneath the waves, in exchange for an extraordinary First Age daiklaive. The final year approaches, and the conclave at Champoor seeks its last 10 victims and a safe means of transporting their patron’s prize back to the Last Bastion.

him to return home. The newest envoy, the young vaktri Koru Mahktran, has earned a tentative place in the Last Mountain’s city by embracing some of his paranoia. They now walk a fine line between two masters, assisting Aton’s priests while assuring the mineral court they’re making progress in calming the rogue elemental.

### Quaghead Tribe, Saints of Thuvaraiyam

Quaghead Tribe,

Saints of Thuvaraiyam

Background: Tattooed thugs and honor-bound heroes, shiftless anarchists and romantic wanderers — the Crocodile Knights are called many things across the wetlands up and down the Grey River, and “Quagheads” is among the more polite. They rove between villages on crocodile-drawn watercraft, unload illegal cargo and intriguing boasts at every stop, and adhere to an opaque charter of strict taboos.

When the Realm’s jade counters call them smugglers, the Quagheads brag about the vital supplies and little luxuries they towed from dangerous ports. When the River Province decries their toll-keeping, the Crocodile Knights take care to escort fisher-rafts through treacherous waters. And when the wars of pious princes flood the Scavenger Lands with refugees, the Saints of Thuvaraiyam welcome them in with smiling jaws. Each chapter of Quagheads wears this rugged mystique like armor, bound together by a secret history whose details are laid bare only to their inner circle. They are no

mere vagabonds, they claim, but heirs to an ancient and noble legacy: Thuvaraiyam, the Land Lost to Law. Most Quagheads consider Thuvaraiyam a mythical ideal of their chivalric charter, passed down through rumors and drunken arguments, but it is quite real. An ancient dominion with a legal code so refined it carved itself on the face of nature, Thuvaraiyam ejected its own populace centuries ago, condemning them as criminals one and all. These banished children are the Saints of Thuvaraiyam, barred from their promised land by a canon lost to time. Now, the dominion’s sole living inhabitants are the Ghara Hags, a sorcerous coven who guide its descendants according to legalistic revelations. They puzzle over mossstrewn mosaics to glean secrets of old, and demand that aspiring chapter masters answer 54 questions of sacred jurisprudence. The Knights observe a motley reconstruction of a numinous charter, and respect no lesser law laid down by god or mortal.

EX3

Merit. In the South, six-legged sand swimmers lurk beneath the dunes. They trade Night Vision for Earthsight (Exalted, p. 521), and use their Swimming pool and Merits to burrow instead.

### NEW GAMBIT: DETONATE (DIFFICULTY 4)

A detonate gambit requires an attack with a firewand, burning arrow, or other fiery weapon against a volatile target like a gas pocket or firedust barrel. Success detonates it, creating a one-time environmental hazard out to short range (damage 4L/instant, difficulty 5).

Members: The Crocodile Knights divide into chapters, each with its own garish heraldry and winding, river-bound territory, answering to a regional Great Chapter led by a Saint. Entrusted with secluded bayou-shrines and the right to interpret Thuvarai law, the Saints lead yearly pilgrimages to the jungle temple where the Knights share business and rumor. This journey requires ritual precision, for the slightest misstep could violate an obscure convention and rouse the land’s elemental enforcers. Only the Ghara Hag shamans can soothe them, and those strange sorcerers seldom venture beyond the holy courthouse where they quiz and anoint each new Saint, except to dredge shattered crystal tablets from Thuvaraiyam’s sodden, haunted archives. Their large, sunken eyes are golden with black sclera, a trait the Knights’ oldest families commonly share. The Knights recruit from runaways and renegades around the near East, but every chapter plasters its boats and bodies with bright heraldry, taking pride in intricate tattoos, styled hair, and articles of crocodile teeth or hide. It’s customary for Knights to trade swaggering stories of how they valiantly won these trophies — and some are even true.

Quagheads ride flat-bottomed vessels shared by two or three boaters, who decorate hulls with painted grins and scales or gator-god figureheads. Boxy keelboats with fluttering pennants carry heavier cargo, and are recycled into lumber at journey’s end. Trained crocodiles can be spotted among these armadas, wearing collars and sunning on rafts, or drawing small canoes with distressing speed. Skilled Knights even harness and ride large crocodiles, amphibious terrors in weed-choked waters. For an example Quaghead adversary, see Bosocu, Prodigal Son of the Swamps (p. XX). Seasoned Quagheads use the traits of grizzled brigands (Exalted, p. 497) with the Strong Lungs and Iron Stomach Merits. Crocodiles are river dragons (Exalted, p. 571) with halved soak and Hardness, −4 damage (and minimum damage) on their bite attack, and no Legendary Size

A crocodile large enough to ride only offers mounted combat benefits while swimming. Its Speed Bonus is +0 on land, +1 in water. A crocodile-drawn boat gains +1 Speed. When ramming, the crocodiles add one to the extra Hull damage inflicted by the Naval Maneuver total, devouring sailors who would normally clamber back aboard. A crocodile-vessel without a ram does not suffer this extra damage.

Secrets: Unbeknownst to the Knights’ northernmost chapter, one of the runaways they recruited recently is a Dynast who smuggled herself off the Blessed Isle to escape assassins and legal trouble. Those things are following her, though, even into the swampy wilderness, and soon anyone in the chapter’s orbit will be dragged into a petty Dynastic conflict.

Resources: Billhooks and chains are Quaghead signatures, the former for hacking through mangrove and uncooperative rivergoers, the latter for securing and boarding boats. Each chapter has its specialties — the Snakefangs use blowguns to silently claim bounties, and the southerly Sand Swimmer chapter buys support from chapter masters with firewands and waterproofed powder-pouches, prestige symbols that prove especially deadly in areas ripe with swamp gas.

The Knights run smuggling rings, collect protection money, and provide violence-for-hire — all completely legal, according to their charter — but their most profitable resource is a grapevine of rumors that runs from one end of their territory to another. Chapters mingle for contests, festivals, and trade, spreading gossip like a rash. If it happened, chances are a crocodile-riding bandit can be found who’ll boast she did it herself. Roleplaying Hints: Quagheads are boisterous and unruly, quick to sound off their chapter’s virtues; they’re the dread of conscientious peacekeepers throughout the East. Every stop is a chance to impress pretty people, offload dangerous junk, and generally take advantage of the fact that they’ll be gone tomorrow — why waste time with local niceties? The charter is all they need! For most, it’s all they have.

Knights typically meet individual insults with thickskinned bluster, but repay those who besmirch chapter or charter with retribution that is disproportionate, enthusiastic, and (if the Knights have their way) legendary. A teahouse owner can toss an unruly Quaghead onto the

EX3

It looks like an enormous tree fern twice a human’s size, dangling heavy fronds lined with waxy, mud-brown leaves over its massive, hoary face to hide it until called upon to show itself. It can move, pulling up slimy rootlegs from the muck, but prefers to plant itself in place and stand sentinel over some precious resource, whether it’s as mundane as clean water or as mystical as a lost demesne. Travelers earn access to its territory by performing tasks it sets that prove they possess an understanding of its age-old wetland wisdom and a healthy respect for nature.

### EXAMPLE CHAPTER:

### THE RUBY JAW RIDERS

Saint Saflonu, whose maimed lower face and crimson fang-pennants are known across the Hundred Kingdoms, leads the Ruby Jaw Riders, a Great Chapter that roams into the Evensong Line whenever Lookshy’s patrols relax. Lips painted with red amaranth, hair styled into crimson spikes, the Jaws traffic in narcotics and exotic entertainment, playing competing interests against each other to avoid outright war with the Guild. Its most notable bylaws are: •

Eat no flesh from a hooved beast

•

Kill no one who has yet to speak

•

Dancers shall receive full hospitality and conveyance

The many zabbala that grow in and around Thuvaraiyam made ancient pacts with the original Ghara shamans there, swearing to stand guard and enforce the complex web of laws until the land itself perished in fire or famine. Not even the Hags know all those laws now, so dealing with the zabbala is a dicey prospect; but those who appease them gain access to Thuvarai secrets and tidbits of a lost history.

street without incident, but one who bars the chapter may find his business demolished overnight. When bitten, the Crocodile Knights bite back harder. Successful chapter masters cultivate abettors in their regular haunts through bribery or intimidation, juggling respect, fear, and disdain with Knightly showmanship. A chapter that sparks a riot might turn it into an impromptu carnival, while one accused of foul crimes sweeps the swamps and returns with the true culprit — or an acceptable scapegoat. The Thuvaraiyam charter’s shared clauses lay out exchange rates for repayment, rivergoing rights-of-way, and proper treatment of crocodiles. Chapter-specific clauses are more idiosyncratic but equally sacrosanct, which causes occasional friction. Disputes over one interpretation or another can be cause for a leadership challenge or turf war.

River trials are a common outlet for these rivalries, for victory in a good clean (relatively, anyway) race is a reliable way to earn a chapter’s respect. The Bloody Regatta of Descending Wood is a sight to behold, sponsored and policed by the Fourfold Malison chapter. Vessels of all kinds race toward Great Forks down the Grey River, employing merciless tactics in pursuit of the grand prize.

Zabbala, the Sentinel Fern

Found in long-forgotten swamplands and dank sewers, the wood elemental known as the zabbala grows anywhere warm, wet, and devoid of too many prying eyes.

### COMBAT

Attack (Fronds): 9 dice (Damage 10) Tags: Bashing, Brawl, Grappling, Natural

Evasion 2, Parry 5

### MERITS

Swamp Camouflage: The zabbala doubles 9s on Stealth rolls. While unmoving and hiding its face with its fronds, it doubles 7s and may hide in plain sight without penalty, pretending to be a simple plant. Hide of Fronds: The zabbala’s −0 health levels represent the heavy fronds that shield its core bulk, and grant it equal soak. It heals these health levels (and regains lost soak) at a rate of one every minute as the fronds grow back, if it has sufficient water and at least a little sunlight.

### OFFENSIVE CHARMS

Sentinel’s Frond Dance (5m, 2i; Simple; One scene; Essence 2): Whipping its heavy fronds out

from its bulk, the zabbala may force any opponent at close or short range to successfully disengage before moving further away, and reduces the Initiative cost of any gambit that would prevent an opponent from reaching a destination within long range of it by one, to a minimum of one. This Charm ends if its last −0 health level fills with damage.

Warden of the Lost Law (10m; Reflexive; Instant; Decisive-only; Essence 3): When rolling Join Battle against any enemy whose Intimacies or motives it has successfully learned via read intentions in the current scene, it may reflexively perform a disarm or grapple gambit against an enemy within close range who’s unaware of its presence before any character acts, waiving the gambit’s Initiative cost. This counts as its attack for the first round of combat.

### DEFENSIVE CHARMS

Unbroken Root Endurance (4m, 1i; Reflexive; Until uprooted; Essence 2): The zabbala digs its roots into the terrain, gaining +5 soak. It cannot be thrown

or dragged in a grapple, but can’t take movement actions without ending this Charm. If hit with an attack or effect that would forcibly move it, it may negate the movement for two Initiative per range band; if it doesn’t negate all the forced movement, it’s uprooted and this Charm’s effect ends.

### MISCELLANEOUS CHARMS

Fern-Seed Blessing (6m, 1wp; Simple; Indefinite; Eclipse; Essence 3): Ordinary ferns don’t have seeds, but the zabbala can compress its Essence to spit out a seed that blesses anyone who possesses it with invisibility and magical protection as long as they’re within its territory; as soon as they leave, the seed loses its power and this Charm ends. The blessed gain double 7s on Stealth rolls when the opposition would use sight to detect them, and +2 to Defense, as long as they remain within the area the zabbala guards. They also gain double 7s to mundane Awareness, Investigation, or Survival rolls to navigate the area or find a particular resource, such as water, food, or treasure.

### Scarlet Egg Players

Background: The chosen of the Dragons are eclectic in their desires and dreams. While most seek positions in the Ministries, the legions, or the Immaculate Order, still others find their passion in the arts. Mortals lucky enough to witness Exalted troubadours’ performances cherish the opportunity as something to tell their children and grandchildren about. The Scarlet Egg Players are one of the few troupes of Dragon-Blooded actors to operate deep in the Threshold.

It includes absolutely no Exalted members. Clever actors use makeup, effects, and impeccable technique to feign the power of the Realm’s Exalted and reap the benefits of being recognized as such. While exceptional mortal thespians may eat at the same table as the royalty they’ve entertained, the jewels of the Scarlet Egg Players eat better than the kings who feed them, and luxuriate in the gifts their hosts shower upon them. Hasan Jasper, a patrician, takes credit for the deception and the troupe’s name, using a clear reference to the Scarlet Empress as a way to deter questions and imply Imperial support. His greatest fear is that if the troupe comes across an audience that would know the difference between their tricks and a true Dragon-Blooded, the Players’ reputation could be ruined. More importantly, his own fortune would vanish. Jasper steers the troupe

clear of cities with satraps and Realm garrisons, avoids Lookshy, and even keeps tabs on rumors of Immaculate monks and wandering heroes to make sure no one has a chance to compare his actors to the real thing. The more success they experience, the bolder the Scarlet Egg Players become and the safer they feel to make ever more ridiculous demands. More than once, the Players have started wars between petty kings and reaped the rewards of putting on a show for the winner. Jasper and his favored members of the troupe all wear gaudy ornaments taken from those who attend their shows. Some of the actors even make veiled threats as to what might happen to someone who refuses to hand over a “gift”. Members: To keep the secret safe, the Scarlet Egg Players only employ the most talented and ambitious actors, playwrights, stagehands, and artisans. Inducting a new member requires a majority vote by the inner circle — composed of those who play the Dragon-Blooded — though Jasper is known to throw his weight around to ensure his favorite candidates are admitted. Most who fail Jasper’s tests of secrecy and loyalty are simply left behind, but the patrician has killed more than one actor who knew too much to let leave.

Secrets: Recently, the Scarlet Egg Players began experimenting with Anathema disguises to beguile gods and

EX3

others who may be amiable to those returning monsters. This frightens Jasper even more than the reward entices his greed, but Twinkling Starry Eyes — who dreamed up the new scheme — is gaining support among the Players’ inner circle. Her endgame would set up the troupe as masters of their own kingdom where they can retire in wealth and safety, discarding their stage personae. Resources: Because of their talents, the Scarlet Egg Players boast more capital than some small Threshold nations. They spend much of it repairing their caravan, feeding themselves, and purchasing paints, dyes and props for their plays. A significant amount of their profits goes toward maintaining their charade and keeping their suppliers ignorant of their clients’ identities. Wood Aspect impersonators adorn themselves with fresh flowers or bathe in fragrant and expensive floral perfumes, while Water Aspect impersonators in hot climates spend a great deal of time oiling their bodies and hair. These unusual materials are obtained through dozens of proxies and businesses owned by characters invented by Jasper, which keeps him essential to the Players’ continued success. Roleplaying Hints: The actors of the Scarlet Egg Players are all exceptional, but only the best and most trusted

actually act as Dragon-Blooded, or even know that the troupe’s Exalted are fakes. They are happy to indulge in the trappings of high society as long as it’s at the expense of their host. The actors train extensively to make sure that their mask never slips, even if they’re intoxicated or otherwise not at their best. Many of the Players have never seen a Dragon-Blood up close, and instead rely on folklore and Jasper’s tales of the Blessed Isle to maintain their image and inspire their depiction of Terrestrial Exalted Charms and sorcery.

Hasan Jasper

Background: As clever as he is ruthless, Jasper was raised on the Blessed Isle to a family with weak blood. Though his parents hoped that their son would be the one to Exalt after generations without the blessing, with every year Jasper became more cynical. At sixteen, he left home, following his dream of becoming a world-renowned actor, leaving behind his childhood friends who had been Chosen when he was not.

The reality of acting in worn-out inns for a place on the kitchen floor and a hot meal set in quickly for Jasper, who again was seized by despair when a year passed and he was still not rich or famous. As his story goes, one night after too

EX3

much wine, he came up with the idea and gathered a few actors he could trust to start the Scarlet Egg Players. Image: Tall and toned, Jasper never breaks character as “Cathak Jasper.” He dresses in bright red flowing robes, and ties his black hair back in a long tail that shows off the dyed red streaks. He uses a combination of makeup and sun to cultivate a reddish tan and even rubs his body with charcoal and firedust to create his character’s scent. Both n and out of character, Jasper wears a sardonic, malicious smirk. Intimacies: Defining: High Society (fascination) Major: “Success belongs to he who takes it.” Minor: “Anyone who we trick should blame themselves for being fools.” Secrets: Jasper has never told anyone his heritage, not even his most trusted partners.

Equipment: Jasper dresses in only the greatest finery and keeps a lion’s share of the profits for himself. Though he could never bear to wear armor, Jasper keeps flamepieces in his flowing sleeves to conjure flame in performances… and to silence suspected threats when offstage. Roleplaying Hints: Even though he maintains his aloof Dynast persona, Jasper’s sarcastic streak breaks through at inopportune times, though he recovers quickly with a small quip or gentle self-mockery. His greatest fear is encountering someone from the Realm who would see through the Players’ disguises and reveal their farce. Whenever he learns of Realm presence nearby, Jasper takes the Players and flees at the earliest opportunity. Though he isn’t beloved by his troupe, Jasper makes sure they all know what he does for them. He maintains informants to know which kingdoms to avoid and which will be most eager for a performance, as well as managing the supply lines that keep the Players going.

The actor’s art is as integral to their being as the swordmaster’s blade is to hers. When playing a part, the thespian is able to play off any slip of the tongue as merely a joke that the witness didn’t understand and any faux pas as merely a failed joke or social misunderstanding. Observers don’t gain bonus dice to their (Perception + Awareness) when the actor acts “out of character”, though they may still attempt to see through the disguise as normal. This doesn’t prevent anyone from being offended by an unkind word or inappropriate comment.

Twinkling Starry Eyes

Background: Once a simple farmer in the Hundred Kingdoms, Twinkling Starry Eyes’ transcendent ambition told her there was more to life than rural living. She worked as a maid, a bartender, a merchant, and more as she amassed a small fortune for herself, but nothing filled the emptiness she felt. Twinkling Starry Eyes lived like she was half asleep, until the day she stepped on a stage. Acting and putting on a show was like finally waking up after twenty years in a dream. She poured her passion into pursuit of the art and quickly became a mildly famous troubadour. Before her career truly took off, the Scarlet Egg Players welcomed her into their fold. After only a year, Jasper was certain she was trustworthy. Officially, Twinkling Starry Eyes split from the troupe to make her own way. In her place, “Tepet Stella” was born. Image: “Tepet Stella” is almost as obsessed with her role as “Cathak” Jasper is with his, remaining in character even on the road and only being herself when she’s alone in her wagon. She wears pale blues and rich purples predominantly, favoring trousers and shoes that allow for easy movement. Twinkling Starry Eyes has always been as thin as a reed. She performs her tight-rope walks on thin wire as a display of her “Air aspect” abilities. Intimacies: Defining: “I live for the rush of the stage.” Major: “The Players are just the means to my end.”, Hasan Jasper (Distrust) Minor: “Nothing will ever hold me down again.”

Secrets: Twinkling Starry Eyes has a plan to conquer territory and suck it dry for the Players by taking the role of a horrific Anathema warrior queen. Some of the

EX3

troupe’s inner circle are intrigued by her plan, but Jasper is openly critical of their chances. In true Dynast fashion, she’s considering ways she might get rid of Jasper.

guide others to her side. Unfortunately, she’s bad at hiding her boundless ambitions, which Jasper uses against her.

Equipment: Like the other players, Twinkling Starry Eyes dresses in elegant clothes to play the role of “Tepet Stella”. She wears even lighter clothing than the others chosen to play Dragon-Blooded to illustrate her powers as an “Air aspect” meaning that she is often very cold. At times, she carries ice within her clothing to throw out as a display of her powers. She carries no weapons but a simple knife.

She expects to be listened to when she speaks and demands respect and obedience from her lesser actors, but is never cruel or overly aggressive with them. When any of her supporting cast stumbles, she is often there to catch them and offer a stern word to help them improve. After all, she remembers what it was like to be new to the stage. Twinkling Starry Eyes lacks Jasper’s easy way of manipulating and guiding others to do her bidding, instead relying on her force of personality and her ambition to

### The Scorpionfolk

Background: Centuries ago, the Lunar sorcerer Liyas created the scorpionfolk to aid in her ambitions to conquer the Firepeaks. She was both their queen and their general, and under her banner their clans swarmed across the lands, seizing territories and annexing kingdoms. After Liyas’ death — accounts of which are non-existant in scorpionfolk writings — the clans swiftly withdrew from the lands they’d taken. In a matter of a scant few months, they ceased prosecuting Liyas’ wars and returned to their villages.

Today, the scorpionfolk are composed of dozens of clans scattered throughout the Firepeaks. These include the diplomatic Neekhoe; the Lahati, who read the stars; and goat-herding Yime, whose long-haired flocks provide them with milk, cheese, and meat. While the clans mostly keep to their own territories, their merchants travel to cities along the Diamond Road to trade coffee, medicinal herbs, mahogany, mohair, and pelts taken from strange beasts in the Wyld. Lahati astrologers have traveled to An-Teng and the distant Varang City-States, both to decipher nobles’ fortunes and consult with their peers on portentous events.

The Neekhoe scorpionfolk attempt to maintain diplomatic relationships with regional powers, receiving emissaries from cities along the Diamond Road and An-Teng. They oversee an uneasy truce with Janati, the youngest of Yena’s daughters, whose followers claim the foothills on the edge of the Firepeaks. To the north, Irembe raiders pose a problem for scorpionfolk towns, sweeping out from Lake Nyandi to take trade goods and livestock. Thus far, Neekhoe outreach to Golden-Leaf Liseli, asking the shahan-ya to intervene, has yielded no results. Some clan princes have begun discussing the idea of going to war. The hardiest scorpionfolk venture into the Firepeaks’ Wyld areas, drawing on centuries of knowledge passed down from their ancestors’ forays. There, they hunt strange creatures and unique to those chaotic, dangerous lands. Their pelts, bones, and blood fetch a high price from sorcerers and crafters in the lowlands. Tinctures from herbs gathered in these places induce visions for scorpionfolk priests, and feature in alchemists’ formulas. Warriors stand watch on the outskirts of such places, a line of defense against raksha who might set their sights on vulnerable villages.

EX3

Travelers seeking safe passage over the Firepeaks often hire scorpionfolk guides to lead them. Not only must wayfarers beware of treacherous terrain — rockslides, sudden drops, and heavy cross-winds — but the guides know where pumas and bears make their dens as well as where the Wyld encroaches on mountain passes. Though they steer clients around known bandit gangs’ territories, most scorpionfolk guides have at least passable negotiating skills should the need arise. Some scorpionfolk are smugglers themselves, transporting goods from Nandao to Gem faster than those caravans who take the Diamond Road. Northern clans have a relationship with the Jau Deians, providing weapons to underground rebels, or offering refuge to those who flee into the mountains to avoid the wrath of House Ragara’s garrison.

Image: Scorpionfolk have two sets of arms: a lower set with human hands, and an upper set ending in pincers. Barbed stingers tip their segmented tails. Many scorpionfolk bear painted markings on the chitinous plates of their arms and backs, with symbols that signify their clan, its history, and their individual accomplishments. While most scorpionfolk are decorated with natural pigments, warriors’ markings resemble Lunars moonsilver tattoos which bioluminesce in Wyld-light (see p. XX).

Intimacies: Defining: “We are no one’s pawns.” Major: “It’s my duty to protect our villages from the Wyld.” Major Tie: My Clan (Loyalty); Minor Tie: Fair Folk (Distrust)

Typical Individuals: Scorpionfolk have settled throughout the Firepeaks, from high mountain crests down into the foothills. They’re farmers and herders, warriors, crafters, scholars, and diplomats. Though most scorpionfolk spend the majority of their lives near the village where they were born, it’s not unusual for them to travel within their local territory to trade with other clans or help defend a village under siege from bandits or hobgoblins. An apprentice might travel hundreds of miles to study under another clan’s master leatherworker, or spend several seasons learning scorpionfolk history from an elder scholar. A half-dozen pages and scribes frequently attend a seasoned Neekhoe diplomat on her missions to Origin or the City of the Steel Lotus. Nearly all scorpionfolk learn basic fighting and defense tactics; even though they relinquished their creators’ conquests, they didn’t abandon the techniques and strategies their ancestors honed as Liyas’ army. Today, scorpionfolk warriors are formidable combatants — caravans traversing the Diamond Road hire them as bodyguards,

EX3

Reincarnated fifty years ago, Liyas has spent most of her new life far away in the East. However, disturbing memories of her last days as the scorpionfolk’s sorcerer-queen have begun returning to her. She’s taken on a mission for the Silver Pact which brings her to the Southwest, where she intends to venture into the Firepeaks once more.

### SCORPIONFOLK MERITS

All scorpionfolk have pedipalps, and many warriors bear bioluminescent markings on their persons (see Siluris, Scorpionfolk Hero p.XX). In addition scorpionfolk might have the following Merits, as befits their role in the story:

Resources: Scorpionfolk warriors and princes wield weapons handed down from their clans’ champions. Among the finely-wrought panoplies are a few artifact swords and sets of armor made from Wyld-borne materials, though the bulk of the treasures are mundane items made by artisan crafters.

Danger Sense: Gain a bonus die on rolls to detect danger

Direction Sense: The character always knows her orientation relative to the five Elemental Poles, and reduces the difficulty on attempts to navigate toward a fixed, known location or to retrace her steps by 2.

The Red Jasper Palace’s libraries are a trove of information for scorpionfolk scholars. Several of its open-topped towers — where stars and constellations come into sharp view — serve as observatories for astrologers.

Language: Most scorpionfolk speak a dialect of Flametongue. Diplomats also tend to learn High and Low Realm as well, especially when dealing with places like An-Teng or Jau Dei.

and mercenary captains offer hefty sums to attract them to their rosters. Around the Firepeaks, young fighters venture into the Wyld to earn their peers’ respect and prove their bravery. Some turn their knowledge into lucrative careers, returning from the middlemarches laden with rare goods and materials.

Clan princes gather several times each year, in Liyas’ opulent Red Jasper Palace on the southern peaks. There, they discuss their peoples’ needs, significant changes or happenings in any Wyld areas their hunters venture into, portents for the upcoming seasons, and other pressing issues. Since the sorcerer-queen’s death, scorpionfolk scholars, priests, and seers have overseen the palace’s upkeep, and claimed its libraries, observatory, and ritual spaces for the clans’ benefit.

Secrets: A year before the end of her reign, a small group of scorpionfolk princes implored Liyas to scale back her ambitions for empire. Despite successful conquests, the heavy casualties and strain on supplies were too great for the scorpionfolk to bear. Liyas refused their requests. After the Battle of Crimson Sands decimated the Jilir clan, the princes returned to the Red Jasper Palace, accompanied by an honor guard of their best warriors. Liyas died in the ensuing conflict, and the three surviving princes vowed to keep the details of that fatal day hidden from the clans. Today, only a handful of scorpionfolk elders — nearly all of them princes and high priests — know the story.

Though they’re sparsely populated, the clans control several strongholds throughout the Firepeaks. Once teeming with warriors, these places now act as waypoints and trading outposts for scorpionfolk guides and their clients.

Roleplaying Hints: In their villages, scorpionfolk are wary — though not fearful — of outsiders. They politely extend their hospitality, but will rescind it if visitors attempt to take advantage of their hosts’ goodwill. Clans are community-focused, sharing knowledge and resources with those in need. Those who frequently deal with other cultures and polities — diplomats, negotiators, merchants, and the like — keep calm even when their counterparts get heated, and deftly soothe injured egos to keep discussions on track.

When called to fight, scorpionfolk form a battle group with Size 1-2, Average Drill, and Might 1.

EX3

A privateer crew sails the frozen winds of the far North, hunting for riches. Deep beneath the Blessed Isle, a Fair Folk prince plots vengeance from within her crystal palace. Not far from Cynosure, a long-dead ruler returns from the Underworld and rallies his descendants to reclaim their stolen territory. In the East, an ancient god’s Chosen sells powerful artifacts to the highest bidder, while out in the Western oceans, a war god and her bloodthirsty children prowl the angry waters.

From bustling cities to remote villages, from Underworld fortresses to Heaven’s bureaucratic halls, to the Bordermarches of the Wyld and beyond, Adversaries of the Righteous features a collection of full character entries designed for Storytellers to include in their games as antagonists and allies, or to use as examples for creating their own. Mortals, elementals, gods, denizens of the Wyld, the Exalted, and even stranger peoples populate both Creation and the pages of this book, as well as groups and organizations for intrepid Chosen to encounter.
